Complex Power Series and Analyticity [S+N]¶

Power series in $\mathbb{C}$ are like real ones, but with disks instead of intervals. A series $\sum c_n (z - z_0)^n$ has a radius of convergence $R$ (Cauchy-Hadamard, same formula as in real analysis):

$$\frac{1}{R} = \limsup_{n \to \infty} |c_n|^{1/n}.$$

Inside $\{|z - z_0| < R\}$ — the disk of convergence — the series converges absolutely. Outside, it diverges. On the boundary, behaviour is delicate.

A function holomorphic on a disk equals its Taylor series there. We saw the hint in real analysis: $1/(1+x^2)$ has Taylor radius 1 because the complex singularities at $\pm i$ are at distance 1. Now we can finally explain that.

This notebook formalises: holomorphic ⇔ analytic on disks, with all the consequences.

In [31]:
load("numerics")$
load("ax-plots")$

Geometric series in $\mathbb{C}$¶

$\sum_{n=0}^\infty z^n = 1/(1 - z)$ for $|z| < 1$. The disk of convergence is the open unit disk; on the boundary $|z| = 1$ the series diverges (the terms don't go to zero).

In [32]:
z_test : 0.5 + 0.3 * %i$
abs_z : float(cabs(z_test))$
print("z =", z_test, "  |z| =", abs_z)$
N : 50$
running : 0.0 + 0.0*%i$
for n : 0 thru N do running : running + z_test^n$
print("Σ z^n (N=", N, "):", expand(running))$
print("1/(1-z):", expand(1/(1 - z_test)))$

z = 0.3*%i+0.5   |z| = 0.58309518948453
Σ z^n (N= 50 ): 0.8823529411761379*%i+1.4705882352960256
1/(1-z): 1/(0.5-0.3*%i)

Visualising the disk of convergence¶

The set of $z$ where partial sums of a power series stay bounded forms a disk (in $\mathbb{C}$), not an arbitrary set. Plot the values of $|S_N(z)|$ for the geometric series at $N = 50$ as a heatmap-like surface — bounded inside the unit disk, blowing up near $|z| = 1$, hopelessly large outside.

In [33]:
/* The geometric series converges to 1/(1 - z) inside |z| < 1 — plot the
   limit's modulus, clamped to keep the figure bounded near the boundary. */
geom_mod : cabs(1 / (1 - (x + y*%i)))$
ax_draw3d(
  explicit(min(geom_mod, 5), x, -1.4, 1.4, y, -1.4, 1.4),
  title="|1/(1-z)| — finite inside |z|<1, blows up at the boundary",
  xlabel="Re z", ylabel="Im z", zlabel="|f|",
  colorscale="Hot"
)$

Radius of convergence dictated by complex singularities [S+N]¶

Maxima can compute the Taylor expansion of $1/(1 + z^2)$ at $z = 0$. The coefficients run $1, 0, -1, 0, 1, 0, -1, \ldots$ — that is, $c_{2k} = (-1)^k$ and $c_{2k+1} = 0$ — giving radius $R = 1$ — the distance to the nearest singularity, which is $\pm i$ (where $1 + z^2 = 0$).

In [34]:
series_atan : taylor(1/(1 + z^2), z, 0, 12)$
print("Taylor series of 1/(1 + z²):"); series_atan;
print()$
print("Singularities (zeros of 1 + z²):")$
solve(1 + z^2 = 0, z);

Taylor series of 1/(1 + z²):
"Taylor series of 1/(1 + z²):"
1-z^2+z^4-z^6+z^8-z^10+z^12
Singularities (zeros of 1 + z²):
Out[34]:

The singularities at $\pm i$ are at distance 1 from the origin. The series converges on $|z| < 1$ and diverges on $|z| > 1$ — including for real $|x| > 1$, even though $1/(1 + x^2)$ is perfectly smooth on all of $\mathbb{R}$.

In real analysis we couldn't see why the radius was 1. Now we can: the radius of convergence equals the distance from the centre to the nearest singularity in $\mathbb{C}$.

Holomorphic ⇒ analytic on disks¶

A theorem we won't prove here but is the heart of complex analysis: any function holomorphic on an open set is analytic — locally equal to its convergent Taylor series. The proof goes through Cauchy's integral formula (notebook 5).

Consequences:

  • $C^\infty$: holomorphic functions are infinitely differentiable. (One complex derivative ⇒ all of them.)
  • Identity theorem: two holomorphic functions agreeing on a set with a limit point (e.g. an interval, a sequence with limit in the domain) agree on the entire connected component.
  • Liouville: a bounded entire function (holomorphic on all of $\mathbb{C}$) is constant.

None of these holds for real $C^\infty$ functions.

Why $1/(1+x^2)$ doesn't have a real-analogue explanation¶

Real $C^\infty$ allows the bump function $e^{-1/x^2}$ — equal to its Taylor series only at the single point of expansion. Complex $C^\infty$ (i.e., holomorphic) doesn't allow this — the existence of one complex derivative drags everything else along.

The reason is integration: $f'(z)$ existing on a disk lets us write $f$ as an integral of itself around small circles (the Cauchy integral formula), and that integral is itself analytic. So holomorphy chains: derivatives exist, integrals over closed paths are zero, the function is analytic. We'll see this machinery in the next notebooks.

Numerical demonstration: rate of convergence inside R [S+N]¶

Take $f(z) = e^z$ (entire, $R = \infty$). Truncating at order $N$ and evaluating at $z = 1 + i$, the error decays factorially in $N$.

In [35]:
z_eval : 1.0 + 1.0 * %i$
truth : float(exp(1.0)) * (cos(1.0) + %i * sin(1.0))$
print("e^{1+i} (truth) =", truth)$

errs : []$
for N in [2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 14, 18] do block([s, n, err],
  s : 0,
  for n : 0 thru N do s : s + z_eval^n / float(n!),
  err : float(cabs(s - truth)),
  print("N =", N, "  |error| =", err),
  errs : endcons(err, errs))$

e^{1+i} (truth) = 2.718281828459045*(0.8414709848078965*%i+0.5403023058681398)
N = 2   |error| = 0.6040357527098376
N = 4   |error| = 0.055624186521464655
N = 6   |error| = 0.0025420013831730726
N = 8   |error| = 6.888597105761968e-5
N = 10   |error| = 1.2319456177166703e-6
N = 14   |error| = 1.4733869315173022e-10
N = 18   |error| = 6.141450319825084e-15

When boundary behaviour matters¶

On the boundary of the disk of convergence, anything can happen:

  • $\sum z^n$ at $z = 1$: terms don't go to 0; diverges.
  • $\sum z^n / n$ at $z = 1$: harmonic series; diverges.
  • $\sum z^n / n$ at $z = -1$: alternating harmonic; converges to $-\ln 2$.
  • $\sum z^n / n^2$: converges absolutely on the closed unit disk by Weierstrass M-test.

Boundary behaviour ties into Fourier theory and analytic continuation — beautiful but heavy machinery for another time.

What's next¶

We can now talk about Taylor series in $\mathbb{C}$ confidently. The next two notebooks build the integration machinery — contour integrals, Cauchy's theorem, and Cauchy's integral formula — that lets us prove the analyticity claim and unlocks the residue calculus.

Reference¶

Stein, E.M. and Shakarchi, R. (2003) Complex Analysis. Princeton Lectures in Analysis II. Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press — ch. 2, Cauchy's Theorem and Its Applications (power series and analyticity).
